The problem is just that while I'm sitting there, physically totally relaxed, my brain is just sort of drifting off into a thousand different directions. I'll be sitting there thinking about, I don't know, whether or not I should grade those essays on the Industrial Revolution tonight or tomorrow, and then that makes me think about how crazy it is that the cotton gin completely changed the trajectory of the American South, and then I'm wondering if I have any cotton shirts that need to be ironed, and then I'm trying to remember if I even own an iron anymore because I think my girlfriend took it to her sister's house last month. \n\nBut none of that feels like restlessness, you know? It's not this agitated state where I feel like I'm trapped or I can't unwind. It's just sort of this constant, quiet hum of thoughts that I don't really have control over. I can sit still, I can relax my body, I just can't seem to get my brain to stop wandering off down these completely random rabbit holes. So, no, I wouldn't say I have that inner restlessness or that feeling of not being able to relax. It's really just the lack of focus, not a feeling of being wound up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these attention problems outside of work, too, like at home or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, oh yeah, it’s... it’s definitely not just a school thing. It’s everywhere. I mean, at home, it’s gotten to the point where my wife—and I know I said girlfriend earlier, we actually just got married over the summer, so I’m still completely getting used to saying 'wife'—but she’s basically had to take over all the household management stuff. \n\nShe does all the bills now, all the scheduling, all the reminders. And it’s not because she wants to, you know? She has a demanding job of her own. But she just... she can’t count on me to keep track of them anymore. Like, a couple of months ago, I was supposed to pay the water bill. I had the tab open on my laptop for literally three weeks. Every single time I opened my computer, I’d look at that tab and think, \"Right, I just need to go grab my checkbook and put the routing number in.\" And then I’d get distracted by an email, or I’d start researching something for a lesson plan, or I'd go to the kitchen for water and forget I was even on the computer, and I just never did it. We actually got a final shut-off notice in the mail. After that, she just sat me down, changed all the login passwords, and said she’d handle it from now on. Which makes me feel terrible, obviously, because I’m a thirty-one-year-old man, I should be able to pay a basic utility bill without my wife having to rescue me. \n\nAnd, honestly, it’s bleeding into everything. Even though I love the actual teaching part of my job, the administrative side? The follow-through? I am just completely dropping the ball, and it’s getting bad. It’s actually become serious enough that my department chair had to pull me into his office recently. He sat me down—and he’s a great guy, Mr. Davis, really supportive—but he had this whole list of things I’d missed. Progress reports I didn't send out, IEP accommodations I forgot to log, parent emails I opened, meant to reply to, and then just completely blanked on. So he actually put me on a formal support plan. \n\nWhich, you know, is basically just a nice, bureaucratic school-district way of saying I’m a liability and they need to micromanage me so I don't screw up the paperwork. It’s incredibly humiliating, if I'm being honest. Having to check in with him every single Friday afternoon just to prove I’ve updated my gradebook and responded to parents? It feels like I’m one of my own sophomore students who forgot to do their homework. So, yeah, whether I'm at home or at work, it just feels like this constant cycle of dropping things and hoping someone else catches them before it all falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of trouble with hyperactivity or impulsive behavior when you were younger?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying earlier about being a statue in Mr. Harrison’s class, the hyperactivity really wasn't there at all. And I wasn't impulsive, either. I wasn't the kid blurting out answers out of turn or jumping off the top of the playground equipment without looking. I was way too in my own head for that. But I definitely had trouble... it was just a completely different kind of trouble. \n\nI actually know this for a fact because my mom is one of those people who saves absolutely everything, and she has this massive, overstuffed accordion folder in her basement with all my old school records. And even way back in primary school, like first, second, third grade, the comments on my report cards were always exactly the same. The teachers would always start with something nice to soften the blow, you know? But then it would invariably say things like, “bright but careless.” That was a favorite phrase. “Careless.” Or they’d write out these long notes about how I “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised.” Because if a teacher wasn't literally standing right over my shoulder, tapping my desk to bring me back to earth, my brain was just gone. I was off in my own little world.\n\nAnd the backpack situation... oh man, my backpack was a complete disaster zone. A black hole. My parents used to dread going through it. My mom would sit me down at the kitchen table on a Friday afternoon and literally just dump the entire contents of the bag onto the table. And every single time, without fail, my parents were always finding these worksheets just completely crushed and crumpled up at the very bottom of my backpack, usually shoved under a stray juice box or a crushed permission slip. And the most frustrating part for them was that a lot of the time, the worksheets were actually finished! I had sat there at home, done the math problems, put the paper in my bag... and then somehow, between the front door of my house and the turn-in basket in the classroom, the assignment just ceased to exist in my reality. I had just completely forgotten to turn it in. \n\nIt drove my parents absolutely crazy, because they couldn't understand how someone could do the work and then just forget the final step. So, no, I wasn't running around the room or acting out impulsively. I wasn't a behavior problem in that sense. But I was definitely still struggling right from the start, just... quietly failing to manage the basic, everyday things that all the other kids seemed to do on autopilot.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, were these attention and organization problems causing clear problems in more than one setting, like both at school and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, it was definitely both places. It wasn't just a school thing, like I was saying with the backpack. Home was just as much of a struggle, if not more, because my parents were the ones who had to deal with the fallout every single day. \n\nI remember... God, I must have been in fourth grade? Maybe fifth? Anyway, my dad got so frustrated with me constantly losing my jacket. It was this really nice, heavy winter coat, navy blue with these bright yellow stripes down the sleeves. And I think I lost it three times in one month. I'd leave it on the bus, or I'd leave it on the playground, or I'd just take it off in the cafeteria and walk away. And my dad, he finally just snapped one day. He sat me down and he was like, \"If you lose this coat again, you're paying for the next one out of your allowance.\" Which, when you're ten and your allowance is like, five bucks a week, is a pretty terrifying threat. \n\nBut it wasn't just the big things like the coat. It was the everyday stuff, too. Like, my room was always a complete disaster area. My mom would tell me to clean it, and I'd go in there with the best of intentions, I really would. I'd start by picking up a book off the floor, and then I'd start reading the book, and then two hours later she'd come in to check on my progress and I'd be sitting in the exact same spot, surrounded by the exact same mess, halfway through a Harry Potter book. \n\nAnd then there were the chores. We had this chore chart on the fridge, and my job was to take out the trash on Tuesday nights. It wasn't a hard job. But I swear, every single Tuesday night, it was a battle. My mom would remind me at dinner, she'd remind me after dinner, she'd remind me before bed. And I'd always say, \"I'll do it in a minute, I just have to finish this one thing.\" And then I'd completely forget. And then Wednesday morning, the garbage truck would come, and the trash would still be sitting in the kitchen, and my parents would be furious. \n\nSo yeah, it was definitely causing problems at home, too. It was this constant source of tension between me and my parents. They just couldn't understand why I couldn't just do the things I was supposed to do, when I was supposed to do them. And honestly, neither could I. It felt like my brain was just fundamentally broken when it came to that stuff. So it was everywhere, really. School, home... it didn't matter where I was, I was always forgetting something or losing something or just generally failing to keep it together.\n\nDoctor: Were you ever described as being very active or constantly on the go as a child?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, not at all. Like I was touching on a minute ago, it was really the exact opposite for me. If anything, my parents probably wished I was a little more active, you know? Because I was basically a lump. I remember my cousins—I have these three cousins on my mom's side, all boys right around my age—and whenever we had family reunions at my aunt's house, they would be going absolutely crazy. She had this big backyard with a trampoline and one of those plastic slip-and-slides, and they would just be running around, screaming, wrestling, constantly moving from the second they woke up until someone forced them to sit down for dinner. \n\nAnd me? I would find a patio chair, usually one in the shade so I wouldn't get sunburned, and I would just sit there. And the funny thing is, I wouldn't even be reading a book or playing a GameBoy half the time, which is what people usually assume when you're the quiet kid. I would literally just be staring at the grass. Or I'd be watching a line of ants carrying a crumb across the concrete, completely and totally lost in some elaborate daydream. My uncle actually used to joke that someone needed to check my pulse because I could sit in the exact same position for an hour without twitching. \n\nSo, \"constantly on the go\"? Definitely not. I was the kid you could leave in a doctor's waiting room or the lobby of a bank, and you could come back two hours later and I'd still be in the exact same chair, just staring at the pattern on the ceiling tiles. It was almost a running joke in my family how physically inactive I was. Now, my mind? My mind was always going a million miles an hour, jumping from one random thought to another, but my body... no, my body was practically in hibernation for most of my childhood. I was never, ever the kid bouncing off the walls.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a hard time waiting your turn?\n\nPatient: No, I can't really say that I have. I know a lot of people get incredibly frustrated with that kind of thing, but for me, waiting is honestly just... it's almost like a free pass to check out for a few minutes. I don't get antsy at all. \n\nLike, my wife, for example, if we're at the grocery store and the person in front of us is paying with exact change, or they need a price check on a head of lettuce or something, she will physically start vibrating with impatience. She'll be sighing, shifting her weight, looking around for another lane to jump into. But me? I'm completely fine. I'll just stand there, staring at the rack of chewing gum or the tabloid headlines, but not even really reading them, you know? Just completely lost in my own thoughts. \n\nIt's the same thing at work. I teach high school—tenth-grade English—and the line for the copy machine in the staff room before first period is notoriously brutal. You've got all these teachers who are highly caffeinated and stressed out, tapping their feet, checking their watches every five seconds because the bell is going to ring. And if someone is printing a massive hundred-page packet, you can just feel the tension in the room skyrocketing. But I just lean against the wall and sort of... drift away. I'll start thinking about how I'm going to explain the themes in *To Kill a Mockingbird* that afternoon, or I'll remember a documentary I watched three years ago about deep-sea marine life, and suddenly fifteen minutes have gone by and I haven't even noticed that I've just been standing there doing absolutely nothing. \n\nSo, no, the whole \"waiting your turn\" thing has never been a struggle. I don't feel that urge to push ahead or interrupt people. If anything, people probably have to remind me that it *is* my turn because I've completely forgotten that I was even waiting in a line to begin with. I'll just be standing at the deli counter, miles away in my own head, until the guy behind the counter has called my number three times and the person behind me has to nudge my shoulder. So yeah, definitely not an issue with impatience or jumping the gun. I'm perfectly content to just stand there and be in my own little world.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I mean, it’s kind of like what I was saying about when I was a kid. The physical restlessness just isn't there for me at all. 

Like, as a teacher, we have these professional development days a few times a year, right? And they are notoriously grueling. They usually stick all the staff in the cafeteria or the auditorium, sitting on those terrible, hard plastic folding chairs that haven't been replaced since the nineties, and we have to listen to a guest speaker talk about, I don't know, standardized testing metrics or new curriculum mapping software for like three hours straight. 

And if you look around the room during one of those sessions, you can see all the other adults practically crawling out of their skin. The math teacher next to me will be furiously clicking his pen, the history teacher in front of me will be bouncing her leg up and down so hard the whole row of chairs shakes, and people are constantly getting up to go to the bathroom or get more coffee just to have an excuse to move their bodies. 

But me? I am completely planted. I don't feel that physical itch to get up and pace or stretch my legs. I can sit in that incredibly uncomfortable plastic chair for the entire three hours without so much as shifting my weight. Now, am I actually listening to the presentation? No, probably not. My mind is usually off somewhere completely different. I'll be sitting perfectly still, looking right at the speaker, but internally I'm trying to remember if I took the chicken out of the freezer to thaw, or I'm mentally rewriting a lesson plan on *The Great Gatsby*, or I'm just zoning out completely, thinking about what kind of car I'd buy if I won the lottery. 

But physically? No, there’s no fidgeting. No tapping, no squirming. It's actually kind of a problem sometimes, because I get so still and so quiet that my principal probably thinks I'm paying incredibly close attention, and then later she'll reference something from the meeting and I'll have absolutely no idea what she's talking about because I wasn't present at all. But yeah, the physical need to move around... I just don't get that. My body is perfectly happy just staying in one spot for hours on end, even if my brain has left the building.

It's actually kind of a problem sometimes, because I get so still and so quiet that my principal probably thinks I'm paying incredibly close attention, and then later she'll reference something from the meeting and I'll have absolutely no idea what she's talking about because I wasn't present at all. But yeah, the physical need to move around... I just don't get that. My body is perfectly happy just staying in one spot for hours on end, even if my brain has left the building.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I forget ordinary responsibilities more than I want to admit, like replying to a parent email, paying a bill before the late notice comes, or picking up something my wife asked for on the way home.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in primary school, my report cards said things like “bright but careless” and “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised,” and my parents were always finding worksheets crumpled in my backpack that I had forgotten to turn in.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work, the missed follow-through has become serious enough that my department chair put me on a support plan, and at home my wife has started taking over bills and reminders because she can’t count on me to keep track of them.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably worse for about 11 months, since the school changed the curriculum calendar and added the after-school planning meetings.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S002\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Were you ever described as being very active or constantly on the go as a child?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, not at all. Like I was touching on a minute ago, it was really the exact opposite for me. If anything, my parents probably wished I was a little more active, you know? Because I was basically a lump. I remember my cousins—I have these three cousins on my mom's side, all boys right around my age—and whenever we had family reunions at my aunt's house, they would be going absolutely crazy. She had this big backyard with a trampoline and one of those plastic slip-and-slides, and they would just be running around, screaming, wrestling, constantly moving from the second they woke up until someone forced them to sit down for dinner. \n\nAnd me? I would find a patio chair, usually one in the shade so I wouldn't get sunburned, and I would just sit there. And the funny thing is, I wouldn't even be reading a book or playing a GameBoy half the time, which is what people usually assume when you're the quiet kid. I would literally just be staring at the grass. Or I'd be watching a line of ants carrying a crumb across the concrete, completely and totally lost in some elaborate daydream. My uncle actually used to joke that someone needed to check my pulse because I could sit in the exact same position for an hour without twitching. \n\nSo, \"constantly on the go\"? Definitely not. I was the kid you could leave in a doctor's waiting room or the lobby of a bank, and you could come back two hours later and I'd still be in the exact same chair, just staring at the pattern on the ceiling tiles. It was almost a running joke in my family how physically inactive I was. Now, my mind? My mind was always going a million miles an hour, jumping from one random thought to another, but my body... no, my body was practically in hibernation for most of my childhood. I was never, ever the kid bouncing off the walls.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a hard time waiting your turn?\n\nPatient: No, I can't really say that I have. I know a lot of people get incredibly frustrated with that kind of thing, but for me, waiting is honestly just... it's almost like a free pass to check out for a few minutes. I don't get antsy at all. \n\nLike, my wife, for example, if we're at the grocery store and the person in front of us is paying with exact change, or they need a price check on a head of lettuce or something, she will physically start vibrating with impatience. She'll be sighing, shifting her weight, looking around for another lane to jump into. But me? I'm completely fine. I'll just stand there, staring at the rack of chewing gum or the tabloid headlines, but not even really reading them, you know? Just completely lost in my own thoughts. \n\nIt's the same thing at work. I teach high school—tenth-grade English—and the line for the copy machine in the staff room before first period is notoriously brutal. You've got all these teachers who are highly caffeinated and stressed out, tapping their feet, checking their watches every five seconds because the bell is going to ring. And if someone is printing a massive hundred-page packet, you can just feel the tension in the room skyrocketing. But I just lean against the wall and sort of... drift away. I'll start thinking about how I'm going to explain the themes in *To Kill a Mockingbird* that afternoon, or I'll remember a documentary I watched three years ago about deep-sea marine life, and suddenly fifteen minutes have gone by and I haven't even noticed that I've just been standing there doing absolutely nothing. \n\nSo, no, the whole \"waiting your turn\" thing has never been a struggle. I don't feel that urge to push ahead or interrupt people. If anything, people probably have to remind me that it *is* my turn because I've completely forgotten that I was even waiting in a line to begin with. I'll just be standing at the deli counter, miles away in my own head, until the guy behind the counter has called my number three times and the person behind me has to nudge my shoulder. So yeah, definitely not an issue with impatience or jumping the gun. I'm perfectly content to just stand there and be in my own little world.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, do you often feel the need to move around or fidget when you’re supposed to sit still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it’s kind of like what I was saying about when I was a kid. The physical restlessness just isn't there for me at all. \n\nLike, as a teacher, we have these professional development days a few times a year, right? And they are notoriously grueling. They usually stick all the staff in the cafeteria or the auditorium, sitting on those terrible, hard plastic folding chairs that haven't been replaced since the nineties, and we have to listen to a guest speaker talk about, I don't know, standardized testing metrics or new curriculum mapping software for like three hours straight. \n\nAnd if you look around the room during one of those sessions, you can see all the other adults practically crawling out of their skin. The math teacher next to me will be furiously clicking his pen, the history teacher in front of me will be bouncing her leg up and down so hard the whole row of chairs shakes, and people are constantly getting up to go to the bathroom or get more coffee just to have an excuse to move their bodies. \n\nBut me? I am completely planted. I don't feel that physical itch to get up and pace or stretch my legs. I can sit in that incredibly uncomfortable plastic chair for the entire three hours without so much as shifting my weight. Now, am I actually listening to the presentation? No, probably not. My mind is usually off somewhere completely different. I'll be sitting perfectly still, looking right at the speaker, but internally I'm trying to remember if I took the chicken out of the freezer to thaw, or I'm mentally rewriting a lesson plan on *The Great Gatsby*, or I'm just zoning out completely, thinking about what kind of car I'd buy if I won the lottery. \n\nBut physically? No, there’s no fidgeting. No tapping, no squirming. It's actually kind of a problem sometimes, because I get so still and so quiet that my principal probably thinks I'm paying incredibly close attention, and then later she'll reference something from the meeting and I'll have absolutely no idea what she's talking about because I wasn't present at all. But yeah, the physical need to move around... I just don't get that. My body is perfectly happy just staying in one spot for hours on end, even if my brain has left the building.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel restless inside, even if you stay physically still?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I wouldn't use the word restless. I know I said earlier that my mind goes a million miles an hour, but it's not... it's not an uncomfortable feeling, if that makes sense? 

When I hear the word "restless," I think of feeling trapped, or having this pent-up energy that has nowhere to go, like you're just itching to break out of your own skin. I really don't get that at all. It’s actually funny you ask that, because we just had midterms a few weeks ago. And when you're proctoring a midterm exam for tenth graders, the district rules are incredibly strict. You can't grade papers, you can't read a book, you can't be on your computer or looking at your phone. You literally just have to sit at the desk at the front of the room and watch thirty teenagers take a test for two straight hours. And the silence in the room is just... it's heavy, you know? The only sound is the clock ticking on the wall and maybe some kid aggressively erasing a scantron bubble. 

The other teachers in the department complain about it constantly. We'll be in the breakroom and they'll talk about how proctoring is literal torture, how they feel like they're going crazy inside their own heads, just mentally begging for the bell to ring because they feel so agitated and pent-up just sitting there with their own thoughts. 

But for me? I honestly kind of look forward to it. It’s not agonizing at all. I don't feel that internal tension or that desperation for it to be over. My mind just sort of... detaches and floats off. I’ll spend the whole two hours mentally redesigning my living room, or trying to remember the starting lineup of the 1998 Chicago Bulls, or just playing out these incredibly elaborate, hypothetical scenarios in my head. I'm not fighting some internal urge to escape, and I'm not feeling anxious or restless inside. I'm just comfortably, completely spaced out. 

So, no, I wouldn't say I feel restless on the inside either. It's never a feeling of being trapped or agitated. It's really more like my brain just takes the opportunity to go on a very long, very meandering vacation while my body stays behind.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I'm glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Well, hi, yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess I'm here because... well, I've just been struggling a lot lately at work, and it's starting to really pile up on me. I'm a high school teacher, and, you know, it's a job that requires a lot of behind-the-scenes work. It’s not like I don't care about my job—I actually really love teaching, the kids are great, mostly—but the administrative side of things is just becoming impossible for me to manage. \n\nLike, I'll have this whole plan for my planning period or my evening at home. I'll make a cup of coffee, clear off my desk, get everything set up perfectly so I'm ready to go. I can sit down with a huge stack of student essays that I absolutely have to get through, or maybe a new curriculum document the district sent out that I need to review for the next quarter, and I fully, one hundred percent intend to just work and power through it. \n\nBut then... I don't even know how it happens, honestly, but within ten minutes, maybe even less, I'll suddenly realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page the entire time. My eyes are on the paper, but I'm barely taking in a single word of what's in front of me. Instead, my brain is just off in a million different directions, thinking about three or four completely unrelated things—like what I'm going to make for dinner, or remembering a random conversation I had with a colleague three days ago, or wondering if I need to get my car's oil changed soon. And by the time I snap back to reality, I haven't accomplished anything. It's just so frustrating because I *want* to do the work, I sit down to do it, but my brain just sort of unplugs from what's right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or unable to stay still?\n\nPatient: No, actually... no, not at all. It’s funny you ask that because, well, it’s almost the exact opposite for me. I don’t really get that physical restlessness, you know? Like, I have some students in my classes who are always bouncing their legs under the desks or clicking their pens constantly—which, as a teacher, can be a little distracting sometimes, but I totally get that they just need to move—but for me, I don't do any of that. \n\nIf anything, I get completely, physically stuck. Like I was saying about sitting down with those essays, when I get to my desk at home, I’ll settle into my chair, pull myself right up to the edge of the desk, and I can literally sit in that exact same position for forty-five minutes without moving a single muscle. My girlfriend has actually pointed this out to me before. She'll walk into the room to ask me a question, maybe about what we're watching on TV later or something, and she'll say I look like a statue just frozen in front of the paper. Because physically, I am completely still. It's just... it's the inside of my head that's running a mile a minute. \n\nSo, yeah, no tapping or pacing or feeling like I need to jump up and walk around. I don't have that urge to move. I'm usually just sitting there, totally motionless, just completely lost in whatever random thought took over my brain while my body just stays put.\n\nDoctor: Did these attention problems begin when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, they did. I mean, it’s not like I woke up one day in my twenties and suddenly couldn't focus. It's always been there, just... maybe it looked a little different back then, or maybe the consequences weren't as severe because, you know, when you're a kid, adults kind of manage your life for you. \n\nBut if I really think back to primary school, I can remember it being an issue even then. My parents kept all my old report cards—my mom has this huge plastic bin in her basement with every piece of paper I ever brought home—and if you look at them, the comments from my teachers are almost identical year after year. It was always, always things like, \"He's a bright boy, but he's so careless,\" or \"He rushes through his work and makes silly mistakes.\" \n\nAnd the big one was always about finishing things. Every single teacher wrote some variation of \"Doesn't finish his work unless closely supervised.\" Like, if the teacher was standing right over my shoulder, tapping the desk, I could get the math worksheet done. But the second she walked away to help someone else, I’d be staring out the window at a bird on the playground, or I’d be taking apart my mechanical pencil to see how the spring worked, and the worksheet would just sit there, half-finished. \n\nAnd then there was the whole issue with turning things in. My backpack was basically a black hole. My parents would sit me down at the kitchen table, watch me do my homework, watch me put it in my folder, watch me put the folder in my backpack... and somehow, between the kitchen table and the teacher's desk the next morning, it would just vanish from my mind. My parents were constantly finding these crumpled-up, totally completed worksheets just shoved at the bottom of my bag under a bunch of old snack wrappers weeks after they were due. I had done the work, I just... forgot to take it out of the bag. So, yeah, it’s definitely been a lifelong thing, it’s just that now, instead of a crumpled math worksheet, it’s a stack of junior year history essays, and there's no one standing over my shoulder to make sure I get them done.\n\nDoctor: As a child or adult, did you often act impulsively or without thinking?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, if anything, I’m almost the exact opposite. I overthink things to the point where I basically just paralyze myself. Like, if you ask my girlfriend, she’ll tell you it can take me twenty-five minutes just to pick what we’re going to watch on Netflix on a Friday night because I feel like I have to read all the descriptions, look up the IMDb ratings, see who directed it, and by the time I actually make a choice, it’s almost too late to even start a movie. So, jumping into things without thinking? That’s really just not me at all. \n\nEven when I was younger, I wasn't the kid who was, you know, blurting out answers in the middle of class or jumping off the very top of the jungle gym without looking. I was always the quiet one, the one who was just sort of spaced out in the back row. Like I was saying earlier about those old report cards, the problem the teachers had with me was never that I was doing things recklessly or acting out of turn, it was that I wasn't doing what I was supposed to be doing because my brain was on another planet entirely. \n\nAnd it's the same as an adult. I don't really buy things on a whim or make sudden life changes. If I need a new coffee maker, for example, I won't just go to the store and grab one off the shelf. I'll spend three days reading online reviews, watching videos about how different machines extract the beans, comparing the water temperature consistency... and then I'll probably forget to actually buy the thing anyway because I got completely distracted by some article about the history of espresso machines in Italy. So, yeah, acting without thinking isn't really a problem I've ever had. If anything, I think way too much, it's just... usually about the completely wrong things at the wrong time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt people or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not. I'm actually really careful about that, probably because, well, as a teacher, I see it all the time and I know how disruptive it can be. You know, you're in the middle of trying to explain the causes of the French Revolution, and you've got this whole narrative arc built up, and you're just getting to the Storming of the Bastille, and suddenly a kid just shouts out, \"Wait, did they have guillotines yet?!\" And it's like, okay, great question, I appreciate the enthusiasm, but you just completely derailed the entire train of thought for the other twenty-nine kids in the room. \n\nSo because I'm so hyper-aware of how that feels from the front of the classroom, I'm really, really conscious of not doing it to other people. If I'm in a conversation, I'm usually the one who's maybe a little too quiet, just waiting for a very clear, obvious pause before I jump in. And honestly, half the time, by the time that pause actually happens, I've completely forgotten what I was going to say anyway because my brain drifted off while I was waiting. \n\nLike, my girlfriend and I will be talking about, I don't know, where to go for dinner, and she'll be listing off places, and I'll have a thought about this one Thai place we went to a year ago, but I don't want to interrupt her list. So I'll just hold onto that thought, but then while I'm holding onto it, I start thinking about how we haven't had Thai food in a while, and then I wonder if there's a difference between red curry and green curry, and by the time she finally stops talking and asks what I think, I'm sitting there wondering how they harvest bamboo shoots and I have absolutely no idea what she just said to me. So, yeah, blurting things out isn't really my issue. I'm much more likely to just fade out of the conversation entirely while the other person is still talking.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often fidgety or unable to stay seated when you were supposed to?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, not at all. That’s... I think that’s actually one of the main reasons nobody ever really caught on to what was going on with me when I was younger. Because, you know, when people think of this sort of thing, they picture the kid who’s bouncing off the walls, the one who’s constantly getting up to sharpen his pencil or, I don't know, poking the kid in front of him. But I was never like that. If anything, I was almost unnaturally still. \n\nLike, I can clearly remember being in fourth grade—Mr. Harrison’s class—and we had these attached desk-chair things, you know the ones with the metal bars on the side? And I would just sit there, completely frozen, just resting my chin on my hand, staring at the dust motes floating in the sunlight coming through the window. I could watch those dust motes for what felt like hours, just tracking them, wondering why they moved the way they did. And because I was so quiet, and because I was physically in my seat, the teachers just assumed I was paying rapt attention to whatever they were saying. \n\nI see it now with my own students. I have kids in my sophomore history classes who literally cannot keep all four chair legs on the floor for more than thirty seconds. They’re tapping their feet, they’re getting up to throw away a tiny scrap of paper, they’re asking to go to the bathroom three times a period. And as a teacher, that’s what draws your eye, right? The physical movement. But me, back then? I was a statue. I never felt that... that physical urge to get up and run around. My restlessness was entirely in my head. My brain was sprinting a marathon, jumping from the dust motes to what I was going to have for lunch to a cartoon I watched the weekend before, but my body was just sitting there, totally motionless. So, yeah, staying seated was never the issue for me. It was just keeping my mind in the seat with me that was the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do your attention problems ever come with actual inner restlessness or a feeling that you can’t relax, even if you stay physically still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel restless or like I can't relax. It's more just... well, it's not a physical feeling, and it's not like an anxious, keyed-up feeling either. It's just that my mind is always going. But it's not an uncomfortable going, if that makes sense? \n\nLike, when I get home from school on a Friday, I can absolutely just sink into the couch and physically relax. I don't feel this driving need to be doing something or this tension in my chest like I have to go, go, go. The problem is just that while I'm sitting there, physically totally relaxed, my brain is just sort of drifting off into a thousand different directions. I'll be sitting there thinking about, I don't know, whether or not I should grade those essays on the Industrial Revolution tonight or tomorrow, and then that makes me think about how crazy it is that the cotton gin completely changed the trajectory of the American South, and then I'm wondering if I have any cotton shirts that need to be ironed, and then I'm trying to remember if I even own an iron anymore because I think my girlfriend took it to her sister's house last month. \n\nBut none of that feels like restlessness, you know? It's not this agitated state where I feel like I'm trapped or I can't unwind. It's just sort of this constant, quiet hum of thoughts that I don't really have control over. I can sit still, I can relax my body, I just can't seem to get my brain to stop wandering off down these completely random rabbit holes. So, no, I wouldn't say I have that inner restlessness or that feeling of not being able to relax. It's really just the lack of focus, not a feeling of being wound up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these attention problems outside of work, too, like at home or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, oh yeah, it’s... it’s definitely not just a school thing. It’s everywhere. I mean, at home, it’s gotten to the point where my wife—and I know I said girlfriend earlier, we actually just got married over the summer, so I’m still completely getting used to saying 'wife'—but she’s basically had to take over all the household management stuff. \n\nShe does all the bills now, all the scheduling, all the reminders. And it’s not because she wants to, you know? She has a demanding job of her own. But she just... she can’t count on me to keep track of them anymore. Like, a couple of months ago, I was supposed to pay the water bill. I had the tab open on my laptop for literally three weeks. Every single time I opened my computer, I’d look at that tab and think, \"Right, I just need to go grab my checkbook and put the routing number in.\" And then I’d get distracted by an email, or I’d start researching something for a lesson plan, or I'd go to the kitchen for water and forget I was even on the computer, and I just never did it. We actually got a final shut-off notice in the mail. After that, she just sat me down, changed all the login passwords, and said she’d handle it from now on. Which makes me feel terrible, obviously, because I’m a thirty-one-year-old man, I should be able to pay a basic utility bill without my wife having to rescue me. \n\nAnd, honestly, it’s bleeding into everything. Even though I love the actual teaching part of my job, the administrative side? The follow-through? I am just completely dropping the ball, and it’s getting bad. He sat me down—and he’s a great guy, Mr. Davis, really supportive—but he had this whole list of things I’d missed. Progress reports I didn't send out, IEP accommodations I forgot to log, parent emails I opened, meant to reply to, and then just completely blanked on. So he actually put me on a formal support plan. \n\nWhich, you know, is basically just a nice, bureaucratic school-district way of saying I’m a liability and they need to micromanage me so I don't screw up the paperwork. It’s incredibly humiliating, if I'm being honest. Having to check in with him every single Friday afternoon just to prove I’ve updated my gradebook and responded to parents? It feels like I’m one of my own sophomore students who forgot to do their homework. So, yeah, whether I'm at home or at work, it just feels like this constant cycle of dropping things and hoping someone else catches them before it all falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of trouble with hyperactivity or impulsive behavior when you were younger?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying earlier about being a statue in Mr. Harrison’s class, the hyperactivity really wasn't there at all. And I wasn't impulsive, either. I wasn't the kid blurting out answers out of turn or jumping off the top of the playground equipment without looking. I was way too in my own head for that. But I definitely had trouble... it was just a completely different kind of trouble. \n\nI actually know this for a fact because my mom is one of those people who saves absolutely everything, and she has this massive, overstuffed accordion folder in her basement with all my old school records. And even way back in primary school, like first, second, third grade, the comments on my report cards were always exactly the same. The teachers would always start with something nice to soften the blow, you know? But then it would invariably say things like, “bright but careless.” That was a favorite phrase. “Careless.” Or they’d write out these long notes about how I “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised.” Because if a teacher wasn't literally standing right over my shoulder, tapping my desk to bring me back to earth, my brain was just gone. I was off in my own little world.\n\nAnd the backpack situation... oh man, my backpack was a complete disaster zone. A black hole. My parents used to dread going through it. My mom would sit me down at the kitchen table on a Friday afternoon and literally just dump the entire contents of the bag onto the table. And the most frustrating part for them was that a lot of the time, the worksheets were actually finished! I had sat there at home, done the math problems, put the paper in my bag... and then somehow, between the front door of my house and the turn-in basket in the classroom, the assignment just ceased to exist in my reality. I had just completely forgotten to turn it in. \n\nIt drove my parents absolutely crazy, because they couldn't understand how someone could do the work and then just forget the final step. So, no, I wasn't running around the room or acting out impulsively. I wasn't a behavior problem in that sense. We had this chore chart on the fridge, and my job was to take out the trash on Tuesday nights. It wasn't a hard job. But I swear, every single Tuesday night, it was a battle. My mom would remind me at dinner, she'd remind me after dinner, she'd remind me before bed. And I'd always say, \"I'll do it in a minute, I just have to finish this one thing.\" And then I'd completely forget. And then Wednesday morning, the garbage truck would come, and the trash would still be sitting in the kitchen, and my parents would be furious. \n\nSo yeah, it was definitely causing problems at home, too. It was this constant source of tension between me and my parents. They just couldn't understand why I couldn't just do the things I was supposed to do, when I was supposed to do them. And honestly, neither could I. It felt like my brain was just fundamentally broken when it came to that stuff. So it was everywhere, really. School, home... it didn't matter where I was, I was always forgetting something or losing something or just generally failing to keep it together.\n\nDoctor: Were you ever described as being very active or constantly on the go as a child?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, not at all. Like I was touching on a minute ago, it was really the exact opposite for me. If anything, my parents probably wished I was a little more active, you know? Because I was basically a lump. I remember my cousins—I have these three cousins on my mom's side, all boys right around my age—and whenever we had family reunions at my aunt's house, they would be going absolutely crazy. She had this big backyard with a trampoline and one of those plastic slip-and-slides, and they would just be running around, screaming, wrestling, constantly moving from the second they woke up until someone forced them to sit down for dinner. \n\nAnd me? I would find a patio chair, usually one in the shade so I wouldn't get sunburned, and I would just sit there. And the funny thing is, I wouldn't even be reading a book or playing a GameBoy half the time, which is what people usually assume when you're the quiet kid. I don't feel that urge to push ahead or interrupt people. The math teacher next to me will be furiously clicking his pen, the history teacher in front of me will be bouncing her leg up and down so hard the whole row of chairs shakes, and people are constantly getting up to go to the bathroom or get more coffee just to have an excuse to move their bodies. \n\nBut me? My mind is usually off somewhere completely different. I'll be sitting perfectly still, looking right at the speaker, but internally I'm trying to remember if I took the chicken out of the freezer to thaw, or I'm mentally rewriting a lesson plan on *The Great Gatsby*, or I'm just zoning out completely, thinking about what kind of car I'd buy if I won the lottery. \n\nBut physically? And because I was so quiet, and because I was physically in my seat, the teachers just assumed I was paying rapt attention to whatever they were saying. \n\nI see it now with my own students. I have kids in my sophomore history classes who literally cannot keep all four chair legs on the floor for more than thirty seconds. They’re tapping their feet, they’re getting up to throw away a tiny scrap of paper, they’re asking to go to the bathroom three times a period. And as a teacher, that’s what draws your eye, right? The physical movement. But me, back then? I was a statue. I never felt that... that physical urge to get up and run around. My restlessness was entirely in my head. My brain was sprinting a marathon, jumping from the dust motes to what I was going to have for lunch to a cartoon I watched the weekend before, but my body was just sitting there, totally motionless. So, yeah, staying seated was never the issue for me. It was just keeping my mind in the seat with me that was the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do your attention problems ever come with actual inner restlessness or a feeling that you can’t relax, even if you stay physically still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel restless or like I can't relax. It's more just... well, it's not a physical feeling, and it's not like an anxious, keyed-up feeling either. It's just that my mind is always going. But it's not an uncomfortable going, if that makes sense? \n\nLike, when I get home from school on a Friday, I can absolutely just sink into the couch and physically relax. I don't feel this driving need to be doing something or this tension in my chest like I have to go, go, go. The problem is just that while I'm sitting there, physically totally relaxed, my brain is just sort of drifting off into a thousand different directions. I'll be sitting there thinking about, I don't know, whether or not I should grade those essays on the Industrial Revolution tonight or tomorrow, and then that makes me think about how crazy it is that the cotton gin completely changed the trajectory of the American South, and then I'm wondering if I have any cotton shirts that need to be ironed, and then I'm trying to remember if I even own an iron anymore because I think my girlfriend took it to her sister's house last month. \n\nBut none of that feels like restlessness, you know? It's not this agitated state where I feel like I'm trapped or I can't unwind. It's just sort of this constant, quiet hum of thoughts that I don't really have control over. I can sit still, I can relax my body, I just can't seem to get my brain to stop wandering off down these completely random rabbit holes. So, no, I wouldn't say I have that inner restlessness or that feeling of not being able to relax. It's really just the lack of focus, not a feeling of being wound up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these attention problems outside of work, too, like at home or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, oh yeah, it’s... it’s definitely not just a school thing. It’s everywhere. I mean, at home, it’s gotten to the point where my wife—and I know I said girlfriend earlier, we actually just got married over the summer, so I’m still completely getting used to saying 'wife'—but she’s basically had to take over all the household management stuff. \n\nShe does all the bills now, all the scheduling, all the reminders. And it’s not because she wants to, you know? She has a demanding job of her own. But she just... she can’t count on me to keep track of them anymore. Like, a couple of months ago, I was supposed to pay the water bill. I had the tab open on my laptop for literally three weeks. Every single time I opened my computer, I’d look at that tab and think, \"Right, I just need to go grab my checkbook and put the routing number in.\" And then I’d get distracted by an email, or I’d start researching something for a lesson plan, or I'd go to the kitchen for water and forget I was even on the computer, and I just never did it. We actually got a final shut-off notice in the mail. After that, she just sat me down, changed all the login passwords, and said she’d handle it from now on. Which makes me feel terrible, obviously, because I’m a thirty-one-year-old man, I should be able to pay a basic utility bill without my wife having to rescue me. \n\nAnd, honestly, it’s bleeding into everything. Even though I love the actual teaching part of my job, the administrative side? The follow-through? I am just completely dropping the ball, and it’s getting bad. It’s actually become serious enough that my department chair had to pull me into his office recently. He sat me down—and he’s a great guy, Mr. Davis, really supportive—but he had this whole list of things I’d missed. Progress reports I didn't send out, IEP accommodations I forgot to log, parent emails I opened, meant to reply to, and then just completely blanked on. So he actually put me on a formal support plan. \n\nWhich, you know, is basically just a nice, bureaucratic school-district way of saying I’m a liability and they need to micromanage me so I don't screw up the paperwork. It’s incredibly humiliating, if I'm being honest. Having to check in with him every single Friday afternoon just to prove I’ve updated my gradebook and responded to parents? It feels like I’m one of my own sophomore students who forgot to do their homework. So, yeah, whether I'm at home or at work, it just feels like this constant cycle of dropping things and hoping someone else catches them before it all falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of trouble with hyperactivity or impulsive behavior when you were younger?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying earlier about being a statue in Mr. Harrison’s class, the hyperactivity really wasn't there at all. And I wasn't impulsive, either. I wasn't the kid blurting out answers out of turn or jumping off the top of the playground equipment without looking. I was way too in my own head for that. But I definitely had trouble... it was just a completely different kind of trouble. \n\nI actually know this for a fact because my mom is one of those people who saves absolutely everything, and she has this massive, overstuffed accordion folder in her basement with all my old school records. And even way back in primary school, like first, second, third grade, the comments on my report cards were always exactly the same. The teachers would always start with something nice to soften the blow, you know? But then it would invariably say things like, “bright but careless.” That was a favorite phrase. “Careless.” Or they’d write out these long notes about how I “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised.” Because if a teacher wasn't literally standing right over my shoulder, tapping my desk to bring me back to earth, my brain was just gone. I was off in my own little world.\n\nAnd the backpack situation... oh man, my backpack was a complete disaster zone. A black hole. My parents used to dread going through it. My mom would sit me down at the kitchen table on a Friday afternoon and literally just dump the entire contents of the bag onto the table. And every single time, without fail, my parents were always finding these worksheets just completely crushed and crumpled up at the very bottom of my backpack, usually shoved under a stray juice box or a crushed permission slip. And the most frustrating part for them was that a lot of the time, the worksheets were actually finished! I had sat there at home, done the math problems, put the paper in my bag... and then somehow, between the front door of my house and the turn-in basket in the classroom, the assignment just ceased to exist in my reality. I had just completely forgotten to turn it in. \n\nIt drove my parents absolutely crazy, because they couldn't understand how someone could do the work and then just forget the final step. So, no, I wasn't running around the room or acting out impulsively. I wasn't a behavior problem in that sense. But I was definitely still struggling right from the start, just... quietly failing to manage the basic, everyday things that all the other kids seemed to do on autopilot.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, were these attention and organization problems causing clear problems in more than one setting, like both at school and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, it was definitely both places. It wasn't just a school thing, like I was saying with the backpack. Home was just as much of a struggle, if not more, because my parents were the ones who had to deal with the fallout every single day. \n\nI remember... God, I must have been in fourth grade? Maybe fifth? Anyway, my dad got so frustrated with me constantly losing my jacket. It was this really nice, heavy winter coat, navy blue with these bright yellow stripes down the sleeves. And I think I lost it three times in one month. I'd leave it on the bus, or I'd leave it on the playground, or I'd just take it off in the cafeteria and walk away. And my dad, he finally just snapped one day. He sat me down and he was like, \"If you lose this coat again, you're paying for the next one out of your allowance.\" Which, when you're ten and your allowance is like, five bucks a week, is a pretty terrifying threat. \n\nBut it wasn't just the big things like the coat. It was the everyday stuff, too. Like, my room was always a complete disaster area. My mom would tell me to clean it, and I'd go in there with the best of intentions, I really would. I'd start by picking up a book off the floor, and then I'd start reading the book, and then two hours later she'd come in to check on my progress and I'd be sitting in the exact same spot, surrounded by the exact same mess, halfway through a Harry Potter book. \n\nAnd then there were the chores. We had this chore chart on the fridge, and my job was to take out the trash on Tuesday nights. It wasn't a hard job. But I swear, every single Tuesday night, it was a battle. My mom would remind me at dinner, she'd remind me after dinner, she'd remind me before bed. And I'd always say, \"I'll do it in a minute, I just have to finish this one thing.\" And then I'd completely forget. And then Wednesday morning, the garbage truck would come, and the trash would still be sitting in the kitchen, and my parents would be furious. \n\nSo yeah, it was definitely causing problems at home, too. It was this constant source of tension between me and my parents. They just couldn't understand why I couldn't just do the things I was supposed to do, when I was supposed to do them. And honestly, neither could I. It felt like my brain was just fundamentally broken when it came to that stuff. So it was everywhere, really. School, home... it didn't matter where I was, I was always forgetting something or losing something or just generally failing to keep it together.\n\nDoctor: Were you ever described as being very active or constantly on the go as a child?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, not at all. Like I was touching on a minute ago, it was really the exact opposite for me. If anything, my parents probably wished I was a little more active, you know? Because I was basically a lump. I remember my cousins—I have these three cousins on my mom's side, all boys right around my age—and whenever we had family reunions at my aunt's house, they would be going absolutely crazy. She had this big backyard with a trampoline and one of those plastic slip-and-slides, and they would just be running around, screaming, wrestling, constantly moving from the second they woke up until someone forced them to sit down for dinner. \n\nAnd me? I would find a patio chair, usually one in the shade so I wouldn't get sunburned, and I would just sit there. And the funny thing is, I wouldn't even be reading a book or playing a GameBoy half the time, which is what people usually assume when you're the quiet kid. I would literally just be staring at the grass. Or I'd be watching a line of ants carrying a crumb across the concrete, completely and totally lost in some elaborate daydream. My uncle actually used to joke that someone needed to check my pulse because I could sit in the exact same position for an hour without twitching. \n\nSo, \"constantly on the go\"? Definitely not. I was the kid you could leave in a doctor's waiting room or the lobby of a bank, and you could come back two hours later and I'd still be in the exact same chair, just staring at the pattern on the ceiling tiles. It was almost a running joke in my family how physically inactive I was. Now, my mind? My mind was always going a million miles an hour, jumping from one random thought to another, but my body... no, my body was practically in hibernation for most of my childhood. I was never, ever the kid bouncing off the walls.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a hard time waiting your turn?\n\nPatient: No, I can't really say that I have. I know a lot of people get incredibly frustrated with that kind of thing, but for me, waiting is honestly just... it's almost like a free pass to check out for a few minutes. I don't get antsy at all. \n\nLike, my wife, for example, if we're at the grocery store and the person in front of us is paying with exact change, or they need a price check on a head of lettuce or something, she will physically start vibrating with impatience. She'll be sighing, shifting her weight, looking around for another lane to jump into. But me? I'm completely fine. I'll just stand there, staring at the rack of chewing gum or the tabloid headlines, but not even really reading them, you know? Just completely lost in my own thoughts. \n\nIt's the same thing at work. I teach high school—tenth-grade English—and the line for the copy machine in the staff room before first period is notoriously brutal. You've got all these teachers who are highly caffeinated and stressed out, tapping their feet, checking their watches every five seconds because the bell is going to ring. And if someone is printing a massive hundred-page packet, you can just feel the tension in the room skyrocketing. But I just lean against the wall and sort of... drift away. I'll start thinking about how I'm going to explain the themes in *To Kill a Mockingbird* that afternoon, or I'll remember a documentary I watched three years ago about deep-sea marine life, and suddenly fifteen minutes have gone by and I haven't even noticed that I've just been standing there doing absolutely nothing. \n\nSo, no, the whole \"waiting your turn\" thing has never been a struggle. I don't feel that urge to push ahead or interrupt people. If anything, people probably have to remind me that it *is* my turn because I've completely forgotten that I was even waiting in a line to begin with. I'll just be standing at the deli counter, miles away in my own head, until the guy behind the counter has called my number three times and the person behind me has to nudge my shoulder. So yeah, definitely not an issue with impatience or jumping the gun. I'm perfectly content to just stand there and be in my own little world.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, do you often feel the need to move around or fidget when you’re supposed to sit still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it’s kind of like what I was saying about when I was a kid. The physical restlessness just isn't there for me at all. \n\nLike, as a teacher, we have these professional development days a few times a year, right? And they are notoriously grueling. They usually stick all the staff in the cafeteria or the auditorium, sitting on those terrible, hard plastic folding chairs that haven't been replaced since the nineties, and we have to listen to a guest speaker talk about, I don't know, standardized testing metrics or new curriculum mapping software for like three hours straight. \n\nAnd if you look around the room during one of those sessions, you can see all the other adults practically crawling out of their skin. The math teacher next to me will be furiously clicking his pen, the history teacher in front of me will be bouncing her leg up and down so hard the whole row of chairs shakes, and people are constantly getting up to go to the bathroom or get more coffee just to have an excuse to move their bodies. \n\nBut me? I am completely planted. I don't feel that physical itch to get up and pace or stretch my legs. I can sit in that incredibly uncomfortable plastic chair for the entire three hours without so much as shifting my weight. Now, am I actually listening to the presentation? No, probably not. My mind is usually off somewhere completely different. I'll be sitting perfectly still, looking right at the speaker, but internally I'm trying to remember if I took the chicken out of the freezer to thaw, or I'm mentally rewriting a lesson plan on *The Great Gatsby*, or I'm just zoning out completely, thinking about what kind of car I'd buy if I won the lottery. \n\nBut physically? No, there’s no fidgeting. No tapping, no squirming. It's actually kind of a problem sometimes, because I get so still and so quiet that my principal probably thinks I'm paying incredibly close attention, and then later she'll reference something from the meeting and I'll have absolutely no idea what she's talking about because I wasn't present at all. But yeah, the physical need to move around... I just don't get that. My body is perfectly happy just staying in one spot for hours on end, even if my brain has left the building.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless inside, even if you stay physically still?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't use the word restless. I know I said earlier that my mind goes a million miles an hour, but it's not... it's not an uncomfortable feeling, if that makes sense? \n\nWhen I hear the word \"restless,\" I think of feeling trapped, or having this pent-up energy that has nowhere to go, like you're just itching to break out of your own skin. I really don't get that at all. It’s actually funny you ask that, because we just had midterms a few weeks ago. And when you're proctoring a midterm exam for tenth graders, the district rules are incredibly strict. You can't grade papers, you can't read a book, you can't be on your computer or looking at your phone. You literally just have to sit at the desk at the front of the room and watch thirty teenagers take a test for two straight hours. And the silence in the room is just... it's heavy, you know? The only sound is the clock ticking on the wall and maybe some kid aggressively erasing a scantron bubble. \n\nThe other teachers in the department complain about it constantly. We'll be in the breakroom and they'll talk about how proctoring is literal torture, how they feel like they're going crazy inside their own heads, just mentally begging for the bell to ring because they feel so agitated and pent-up just sitting there with their own thoughts. \n\nBut for me? I honestly kind of look forward to it. It’s not agonizing at all. I don't feel that internal tension or that desperation for it to be over. My mind just sort of... detaches and floats off. I’ll spend the whole two hours mentally redesigning my living room, or trying to remember the starting lineup of the 1998 Chicago Bulls, or just playing out these incredibly elaborate, hypothetical scenarios in my head. I'm not fighting some internal urge to escape, and I'm not feeling anxious or restless inside. I'm just comfortably, completely spaced out. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I feel restless on the inside either. It's never a feeling of being trapped or agitated. It's really more like my brain just takes the opportunity to go on a very long, very meandering vacation while my body stays behind.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, honestly, I really don't. I know we kind of just touched on the fidgeting and the internal restlessness a second ago, but yeah, even looking at the whole picture—the staying seated, the blurting things out, all of that—it's just... it's really not me. I don't have any of that hyper energy. 

Like, with staying seated, for example. We have these parent-teacher conferences twice a year, and they set us up in the main gymnasium, which always smells vaguely of floor wax and old sweat. It's just rows and rows of these little folding tables, and we are expected to sit there from 4:00 PM to 8:00 PM as the parents cycle through. And some of the other teachers, I mean, they literally cannot handle it. You watch them and they're standing up, stretching their backs, walking over to the water fountain every ten minutes, leaning against the bleachers just to change positions. But I will literally sit in that exact same spot, in that same posture, for the entire four hours. I don't even think about getting up. My body just sort of... powers down, I guess? 

And then as for the blurting things out or being impulsive... no, definitely not. If anything, I probably have the exact opposite problem. Like, when I'm leading a class discussion—say we're talking about the symbolism of the green light in *Gatsby*, which we usually do right around November—the kids will be talking, and I'll be listening, but then my brain will just snag on something one of them said. And I'll start following this long mental thread about the American Dream, or the history of the 1920s, or I'll wonder if Fitzgerald actually intended for it to mean all that or if English teachers are just over-analyzing it. And suddenly there's this huge, awkward dead air in the room because I haven't responded to the student who just spoke. 

I never interrupt them. I never just blurt out the answer or talk over people. I'm usually so deep in my own head that I'm the last person to speak up in a conversation, whether that's in the classroom or, you know, at a dinner party with my wife's friends. I'll just be sitting there, perfectly still, while they debate where to go for dinner, and I won't say a word because I'm thinking about something completely unrelated. So, yeah, I don't have that impulse to jump in or interrupt or jump out of my seat at all. It's just not how I operate.

My mom would remind me at dinner, she'd remind me after dinner, she'd remind me before bed. And I'd always say, \"I'll do it in a minute, I just have to finish this one thing.\" And then I'd completely forget. And then Wednesday morning, the garbage truck would come, and the trash would still be sitting in the kitchen, and my parents would be furious. \n\nSo yeah, it was definitely causing problems at home, too. It was this constant source of tension between me and my parents. They just couldn't understand why I couldn't just do the things I was supposed to do, when I was supposed to do them. And honestly, neither could I. It felt like my brain was just fundamentally broken when it came to that stuff. So it was everywhere, really. School, home... it didn't matter where I was, I was always forgetting something or losing something or just generally failing to keep it together.\n\nDoctor: Were you ever described as being very active or constantly on the go as a child?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, not at all. Like I was touching on a minute ago, it was really the exact opposite for me. If anything, my parents probably wished I was a little more active, you know? Because I was basically a lump. I remember my cousins—I have these three cousins on my mom's side, all boys right around my age—and whenever we had family reunions at my aunt's house, they would be going absolutely crazy. She had this big backyard with a trampoline and one of those plastic slip-and-slides, and they would just be running around, screaming, wrestling, constantly moving from the second they woke up until someone forced them to sit down for dinner. \n\nAnd me? I would find a patio chair, usually one in the shade so I wouldn't get sunburned, and I would just sit there. And the funny thing is, I wouldn't even be reading a book or playing a GameBoy half the time, which is what people usually assume when you're the quiet kid. I would literally just be staring at the grass. Or I'd be watching a line of ants carrying a crumb across the concrete, completely and totally lost in some elaborate daydream. My uncle actually used to joke that someone needed to check my pulse because I could sit in the exact same position for an hour without twitching. \n\nSo, \"constantly on the go\"? Definitely not. I was the kid you could leave in a doctor's waiting room or the lobby of a bank, and you could come back two hours later and I'd still be in the exact same chair, just staring at the pattern on the ceiling tiles. It was almost a running joke in my family how physically inactive I was. Now, my mind? My mind was always going a million miles an hour, jumping from one random thought to another, but my body... no, my body was practically in hibernation for most of my childhood. I was never, ever the kid bouncing off the walls.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a hard time waiting your turn?\n\nPatient: No, I can't really say that I have. I know a lot of people get incredibly frustrated with that kind of thing, but for me, waiting is honestly just... it's almost like a free pass to check out for a few minutes. I don't get antsy at all. \n\nLike, my wife, for example, if we're at the grocery store and the person in front of us is paying with exact change, or they need a price check on a head of lettuce or something, she will physically start vibrating with impatience. She'll be sighing, shifting her weight, looking around for another lane to jump into. But me? I'm completely fine. I'll just stand there, staring at the rack of chewing gum or the tabloid headlines, but not even really reading them, you know? I don't feel that urge to push ahead or interrupt people. I'm perfectly content to just stand there and be in my own little world.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, do you often feel the need to move around or fidget when you’re supposed to sit still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. The physical restlessness just isn't there for me at all. \n\nLike, as a teacher, we have these professional development days a few times a year, right? And they are notoriously grueling. They usually stick all the staff in the cafeteria or the auditorium, sitting on those terrible, hard plastic folding chairs that haven't been replaced since the nineties, and we have to listen to a guest speaker talk about, I don't know, standardized testing metrics or new curriculum mapping software for like three hours straight. \n\nAnd if you look around the room during one of those sessions, you can see all the other adults practically crawling out of their skin. The math teacher next to me will be furiously clicking his pen, the history teacher in front of me will be bouncing her leg up and down so hard the whole row of chairs shakes, and people are constantly getting up to go to the bathroom or get more coffee just to have an excuse to move their bodies. \n\nBut me? I am completely planted. I don't feel that physical itch to get up and pace or stretch my legs. I can sit in that incredibly uncomfortable plastic chair for the entire three hours without so much as shifting my weight. Now, am I actually listening to the presentation? No, probably not. My mind is usually off somewhere completely different. I'll be sitting perfectly still, looking right at the speaker, but internally I'm trying to remember if I took the chicken out of the freezer to thaw, or I'm mentally rewriting a lesson plan on *The Great Gatsby*, or I'm just zoning out completely, thinking about what kind of car I'd buy if I won the lottery. \n\nBut physically? You can't grade papers, you can't read a book, you can't be on your computer or looking at your phone. We'll be in the breakroom and they'll talk about how proctoring is literal torture, how they feel like they're going crazy inside their own heads, just mentally begging for the bell to ring because they feel so agitated and pent-up just sitting there with their own thoughts. \n\nBut for me? I honestly kind of look forward to it. It’s not agonizing at all. I don't feel that internal tension or that desperation for it to be over. My mind just sort of... detaches and floats off.
